### `ops/verify-db-indexes.ts [--env {qa|prod}] [--all]` (MONEY-1485)

Asks a database which of the schema's 79 `@@unique` constraints it ACTUALLY
enforces, by reading the generated Prisma DMMF and comparing it against
`listCollections` + `listIndexes`.

**Why it exists.** `putCandles` absorbs a duplicate-key collision correctly — a
colliding `createMany` falls back to per-row and counts a duplicate as a no-op
— and that is right ONLY IF the unique index exists. In QA it did not:
duplicates predated the `@@unique`, so `prisma db push` could not build
`candles_symbol_exchange_timeframe_ts_key`, the index was never created, and
the collision the code is written to absorb never happened. Every duplicate
inserted cleanly and was counted as `created`. It surfaced through a
developer's local `yarn dev`, not through any monitor, test or job outcome.

`prisma db push` is the only thing that builds these indexes and it is a
DEVELOPER action — it appears in `turbo.json` and in hand-run setup scripts and
in NO deploy pipeline, so an environment can run indefinitely without an index
its writers depend on.

**READ-ONLY, with no `--apply` to forget.** It issues `listCollections` and
`listIndexes` and nothing else. It deliberately cannot create an index:
creating one over a collection that already holds duplicates is the operation
that FAILS, and doing that unattended against prod is not something a
verification script should be able to do by accident.

Four verdicts, and the last two are why it is not a boolean: `OK`; `MISSING`
(the QA state — including an index on the right fields that is NOT unique,
which is the most misleading shape because every listing shows an index there);
`WEAKENED` (unique but sparse/partial, so it enforces less than it claims —
and a Mongo `db push` creates indexes and not FIELDS, so rows predating a
column have it ABSENT, which is exactly the population a sparse index stops
covering); and `COLLECTION_ABSENT`, which is NOT a fault — no row can violate a
constraint in a namespace nothing has written to.

Matching is on the SET of fields, not the sequence: a unique index on `(a,b)`
and one on `(b,a)` enforce the identical constraint, and a false MISSING on a
correct database is how an alarm gets switched off.

The audit REFUSES rather than reporting health it did not measure — an empty
constraint list, a collection never looked up, or a truncated
`listCollections` batch each produce a refusal with its own exit code.

Exit codes: **0** sound · **1** MISSING/WEAKENED found · **2** REFUSED. The
third is separate on purpose: "I could not measure" must never share an exit
code with "I measured and it was fine".

Measured 2026-09-19: QA 79/79 enforced, prod 79/79 enforced, 0 collections
absent in either. The same check runs at worker boot (`workers/src/index.ts`,
after `serve()` and never awaited) so it does not depend on anyone remembering
to run it.

### `ops/lake-apply-ddl.ts --env {qa|prod} [--apply]` (MONEY-1472, B1)

```bash
npx tsx scripts/ops/lake-apply-ddl.ts --env prod            # print the rendered SQL
npx tsx scripts/ops/lake-apply-ddl.ts --env prod --apply
```

Applies `packages/lake/ddl/*.sql` to `lake_bronze_{env}`. The files are
templates; `renderDdl` substitutes `{{dataset}}` and **REFUSES to emit SQL still
carrying an unsubstituted `{{…}}`** — BigQuery would otherwise accept a literal
brace in an identifier position and create a table nobody can find.

`splitStatements` is QUOTE-AWARE (`"`, `'`, backtick, escapes) and throws on an
unterminated literal. A naive split on `;` cuts a statement in half the first
time a column comment contains one, and the halves can each parse.

Idempotent — every statement is `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`,
`ALTER TABLE … ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS`, or `ALTER TABLE … ALTER COLUMN … SET
OPTIONS(…)` (MONEY-1522: the only way a live column's description changes,
since `CREATE … IF NOT EXISTS` never touches an existing table), or — for 005 —
a `CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW`, re-applied on every run: a view holds no rows, so
replacing it with itself is the no-op, and MONEY-1602 changed its SELECT. Applied
to both `lake_bronze_qa` and `lake_bronze_prod`.

### `ops/setup-dataform.sh {qa|prod} [--dry-run] [--image=IMG]` (MONEY-1611; was MONEY-1506)

Provisions `lake-gold-{env}`, the Cloud Run job that builds gold by running the
Dataform CLI over the `dataform/` directory baked into the workers image, and
its two triggers: `lake-gold-after-us-{env}` at 06:45 IST and
`lake-gold-after-in-{env}` at 20:15 IST. No managed Dataform repository, no
release config, no Git token — none was ever created. The job builds exactly the
SQL of the release it is pinned to; the release's re-pin loop moves it.

It runs as `lake-dataform-{env}@`, whose gold WRITER / silver READER / jobUser
grants come from `setup-lake.sh`. This script adds only what the job needs:
`DD_API_KEY_{ENV}` secretAccessor, `run.invoker` on the job (the triggers run as
its own SA), and `dataViewer` on the ONE bronze table `partition_load`,
TABLE-level — the job reads the ledger before building and skips
(`SKIPPED_LOADING <table>: <partitions>`) while a silver input is mid-reload —
exit 0 on a scheduled run, exit 1 with a retry time on a targeted one. No key file and no `DATABASE_URL`. Pins the image off the live
workers service and prints its SHA. QA only after the first MONEY-1610 lake copy
(QA's silver DDL was behind prod's). After any historical replay, full-refresh
`event_return_outcome`; the printed commands include it.

### `ops/adjustment-parity.ts {qa|prod}` (MONEY-1506, B4; read-only since MONEY-1528)

```bash
npx tsx scripts/ops/adjustment-parity.ts qa
npx tsx scripts/ops/adjustment-parity.ts prod   # after any silver DDL the SQL names
```

Proves the SQL CRSP adjustment equals the TypeScript **in BigQuery**, with ONE
read-only query. Both fixtures — `fixtures/adjustment-parity.csv` and the
action-path fixture `fixtures/adjustment-parity-actions.ts` — are rendered
INLINE and unioned onto `lake_silver_{env}.<table> WHERE FALSE`, so it bills 0
bytes of silver but still resolves every column against the DEPLOYED schema: a
column the SQL names that this env lacks (`observed_ratio` before its DDL) fails
HERE, by name. It runs `adjustedBarD1Sql`, rebuilds the reference series with
`computeAdjustedSeries` from the raw columns BigQuery returned, compares to
1e-9, and asserts the query ran in asia-south1. **It used to WRITE** — a scratch
table plus a load job in prod silver, kept with `--keep` (now removed) — and its
action side was `corporate_action WHERE FALSE`, so no action CTE ever executed
here. Fed actions, it caught the NUMERIC(38,9) factor quantisation MONEY-1528
fixed.

The CI half —
`packages/core/__tests__/market-data/adjustment-sql-parity.test.ts` — runs the
same SQL over the same fixture in `node:sqlite` on every push and proves the
ALGEBRA (measured worst disagreement 2.8e-16). This script adds the two things
SQLite cannot: that BigQuery PARSES the text, and that its NUMERIC(38,9)
rounding stays inside the bound. It needs credentials, so it is a
`verify:push` proof rather than a test.

### `ops/embeddings-profile.ts --env {qa|prod} [--sample N] [--census]` (MONEY-1471, B0)

```bash
npx tsx scripts/ops/embeddings-profile.ts --env prod --sample 20000
npx tsx scripts/ops/embeddings-profile.ts --env prod --census   # FULL SCAN
```

**READ-ONLY** — writes nothing, deletes nothing, creates no index. Measures
`document_embeddings` for the B8 shrink decision, sizing the vector and content
halves SEPARATELY because each option shrinks one or the other. Findings live in
`docs/reference/embeddings-decision-2026-09.md`; the headline is that the vector
is 3.1x the text, inverting the assumption the work was scoped on.

**Every aggregation is bounded by `maxTimeMS = 120000`.** The first cut had none,
ran twenty minutes against the cluster that serves the product, and had to be
killed. `--census` is the full scan and currently EXCEEDS that ceiling on prod —
it is deferred to B8, which scans the collection anyway.

The cursor is DRAINED, not read as one batch: a `$sample` of 2,000 returned 101
rows in `firstBatch`, so every byte statistic was computed from 101 documents
while the header claimed 2,000.

§7 reads the live Atlas Search index definition through `$listSearchIndexes` — a
native stage on the connection the script already holds, so no Atlas Admin key
pair. It reports **three** states, never two: `DID NOT RUN` with the error,
`none declared`, or the fields. A failed call and an empty list are different
answers, and collapsing them is how a measurement that did not happen gets
written down as a finding.

### `ops/apply-atlas-search-indexes.ts --env={qa|prod} [--apply] [--verify] [--probe]` (MONEY-1499, B8)

```bash
npx tsx scripts/ops/apply-atlas-search-indexes.ts --env=prod             # dry run
npx tsx scripts/ops/apply-atlas-search-indexes.ts --env=prod --apply
npx tsx scripts/ops/apply-atlas-search-indexes.ts --env=prod --verify    # exit 3 on drift
npx tsx scripts/ops/apply-atlas-search-indexes.ts --env=prod --probe --apply
```

Applies `scripts/ops/atlas-search-indexes.json`, which is now the source for
this cluster's Vector Search index definitions. **Until MONEY-1499 they existed
ONLY in the Atlas UI** — no diff, no review, no way to recreate them.

DRY RUN IS THE DEFAULT. `manage` is per index and the two values carry very
different risk: `apply` creates or updates; `verify-only` reports a difference
and changes NOTHING, which is what the v1 index on a 67 GiB collection gets,
because `updateSearchIndex` there triggers a rebuild of the index the research
agent queries inside a chat turn. `drift` is a FOURTH plan state, not a flavour
of `noop` — a live index that no longer matches the file must be visible
without being acted on.

Every definition is validated BEFORE the connection is opened, against the rules
for the dtype the collection actually stores. The one that matters:
**`quantization: "scalar"` over an int8 field makes Atlas silently SKIP every
vector** — the collection still shrinks, the index still builds, `collStats`
still passes the footprint criterion, and retrieval returns nothing.

`--probe` writes ONE synthetic row to its own throwaway collection and answers
the two questions that cannot be answered by reading and are silent when wrong:
does a subtype-9 binary survive `$runCommandRaw` intact, and does Atlas accept a
BinData `queryVector` through it. Run it before the backfill, not after. It
deletes nothing; the cleanup command is printed for a human.

### `ops/embeddings-parity.ts --env={qa|prod} [--out=FILE]` (MONEY-1499, B8)

```bash
npx tsx scripts/ops/embeddings-parity.ts --env=qa
npx tsx scripts/ops/embeddings-parity.ts --env=prod --out=parity.json
```

**READ-ONLY.** Runs the fixed query set in `ops/embeddings-parity-queries.json`
against v1 and v2 in the same process, seconds apart, and judges B0 §6's parity
and latency criteria. The query set is committed BEFORE the migration precisely
so it cannot be chosen to pass; editing it after a run invalidates that run.

Overlap is set-based at top-K with **K in the denominator**, so a v2 returning
fewer rows is penalised — dividing by the smaller set would score a collapsed
index as perfect parity. An empty or short run REFUSES rather than passing:
two averages compared with `>=` make a population of zero read OK for ever. A
result id that does not decode REFUSES too, rather than becoming `""` — the
overlap de-duplicates through a `Set`, so an all-undecoded run would otherwise
score a perfect 1.000 and be the evidence for arming the rename.

**TWO overlap rungs, on DIFFERENT thresholds, and the difference is arithmetic
rather than taste** (MONEY-1499 review). The MEAN must clear 0.9 (B0 §6). The
PER-QUERY rung is stated in DOCUMENTS — at most `PARITY_MAX_MISSING_PER_QUERY`
(1) of the top-K may differ — because `overlapAt` returns `hit / k` and 0.9 is
not on a 6-point grid: reusing it per query made 1.0 the only passing value, so
the gate silently demanded a byte-identical top-6 on all fifty queries. Two ANN
graphs at numCandidates 72 over ~5.9M rows will not give you that, and the only
exits from such a gate are abandoning a correct migration or editing the
constant while staring at the number it refused.

### `ops/setup-embeddings-v2-job.sh {qa|prod} [--image=] [--task-timeout=] [--dry-run]` (MONEY-1499, B8)

Deploys `embeddings-v2-backfill-{env}` off the live `stockbook-workers` image.
**Ships DISARMED** and has no Scheduler trigger — a one-time migration does not
get a cron. Two arm flags, both PRESENT and `"false"`:
`EMBEDDINGS_V2_BACKFILL_APPLY` and `EMBEDDINGS_V2_RENAME_APPLY`, separate
because the steady state is "backfill armed, rename not". Arm with
`--update-env-vars`, never `--set-env-vars` (which replaces the whole block and
would wipe the DD\_\* wiring). The script prints the full order of operations.

### `ops/datadog/apply-monitors.sh {qa|prod} [--dry-run|--only=verify]` (MONEY-1220, repaired by MONEY-1238)

Provisions one zero-write monitor per job in `MONITORED_JOBS`, plus the latency
and absence monitors below. Renders **70 on prod** — 41 zero-write, 27 absence
(18 standing + 9 sparse), 1 latency, 1 tripwire. Counted from the arrays, not
restated: this line read "45 on prod / 13 on QA" across several tickets that
added rows. QA renders the same set and applies NONE of it unless
`DD_QA_MONITORING` is set (`dd_qa_monitoring_enabled` in `lib-datadog.sh`).

**THREE templates now, all committed files** (MONEY-1266/1293/1358).
`monitor-job-absence.json` was a heredoc written to a `mktemp` at run time while
it served one job, with a note to promote it when a second needed it; four jobs
use it now. It asks a question neither other template can: the zero-write query
is a DIFFERENCE of two counters, so a job that never ran emits neither and the
query cannot evaluate at all — `default_zero()` makes silence a real 0.

**THE ABSENCE TEMPLATE HAS TWO KINDS, and the row says which** (MONEY-1399).
Rows are `job|window|kind|cadence`; `kind` is `standing` or `sparse`, the script
exits 1 on anything else, and it picks between two template files:

| kind       | file                              | query                                | window sized by                                                    |
| ---------- | --------------------------------- | ------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| `standing` | `monitor-job-absence.json`        | sums `selected`                      | the cron gap AND the longest legitimate stretch of `selected == 0` |
| `sparse`   | `monitor-job-absence-sparse.json` | counts SUBMISSIONS, `count_not_null` | the cron gap ONLY                                                  |

**The two differ in the QUESTION, never in the alerting behaviour.** Same `name`
— which is load-bearing, because the upsert is by name, so switching a row's
kind PUTs over the existing monitor instead of creating a second one beside it —
same `type`, same `options`, same `critical: 1`.

Which kind a job gets is a fact about its SELECTION, and checkpointing predicted
it perfectly across all 25 rows: a job that checkpoints or gates on novelty
(`edgar-bulk-ftd`, `feed-parse`, `databento-append`, `resend-contact-sync`,
`tiingo-news-poll`, `report-archive-sync`, `candle-evict`, `price-backfill`,
`news-rss-sync`) reports `selected: 0` when it is CAUGHT UP, which is health. A job that re-selects a page every run
(`news-sync`, `price-sync`, `xbrl-ratios`, the exchange-driven five, …) reports
0 only when its gate has broken, which is the defect MONEY-1215 exists for —
those stay `standing` and keep alarming on a zero.

**Datadog already distinguishes the two states; the standing query throws it
away.** Measured 2026-09-13: `edgar-bulk-ftd`'s 2026-09-10 13:30Z run left ONE
non-null point whose value is `0.0`, while a job that did not run has NO SERIES
at all. `default_zero(sum:…)` fills the gaps with zeros and makes them
identical, which is why that monitor was created 2026-09-06, went to Alert 11
seconds later, and had never once been OK while `us_cusip_map.SEC_FTD` (23,406
rows) was being filled correctly underneath it. `etf-expense-enrichment` was
the same shape and worse — its candidate query EXCLUDED every already-enriched
ETF, so a drained queue reported 0 FOREVER — and that exemption also hid the
fact that the job could never write at all (`fetchKuveraMetadata` returns null
unless `MF_METADATA_PROVIDER === "kuvera"`; both specs set `"amfi"`). A
guaranteed no-op and a finished backlog produced the identical signal, which is
why it ran every Sunday since MONEY-209 without anyone noticing. It is RETIRED
in MONEY-1404 and both of its rows are gone from the lists above, but the
LESSON is about the `sparse` exemption rather than about that job: a `sparse`
row cannot tell "caught up" from "broken", so spend it only where the job's
health is established some other way.

`default_zero()` stays in BOTH forms and is load-bearing in the sparse one:
`count_not_null` alone returns NO SERIES on a silent window, so the threshold
would never evaluate and the monitor would sit grey rather than alert.

**`candle-evict` is on the absence list and must not move to the zero-write
one.** It ships disarmed (`CANDLE_EVICT_APPLY="false"`) and a disarmed run
honestly reports `selected: N>0, written: 0`, so a zero-write row would page
every night in the configuration we ship (MONEY-1266).

**`tile-build-d1-in` / `tile-build-d1-us` are absence-only for the same reason,
and an absence row is the ONLY monitor that could have caught what happened to
them** (MONEY-1479). `tile-build-d1-us` was OOM-killed nightly for weeks. A
SIGKILL runs none of our code — `zeroWriteRefusal` never evaluates,
`reportJobOutcome` is never called, `flushMetrics` never posts — so the job
contributes no point at all and SILENCE is the only symptom it leaves. Every
in-process guard these jobs carry is downstream of surviving. They had no row in
any list in this file until now. A zero-write row would PAGE ON A MARKET
HOLIDAY: `written` is `counters.built`, and a session with no new bars advances
no `source_max_date`, so `rebuildReason` returns null for every listing,
everything is SKIPPED, and the run honestly reports `selected: 3492,
written: 0`.

**Windows are checked against the CADENCE, by simulation.**
`monitor-agreement.test.ts` expands each job's crons over a week and asserts the
window exceeds the largest gap. The rule was argued in prose here and applied by
hand, and got three jobs wrong: `bhavcopy-sync` (36h on a `1-5` cron, MONEY-1238),
`feed-parse` and `xbrl-ratios` — the last two found BY the check on the day it
was written. A window under the gap means the series is legitimately empty for
part of every cycle, and with `notify_no_data: true` that is a pager firing on a
healthy schedule. The cron lookup reads all three places this estate provisions
schedules: a `SCHEDULER_DEFS` row by topic, one by entry name (`price-backfill`
rides the `price-sync` topic), and `schedule_job_run` in a job provisioner
(`feed-parse`'s trigger is created by `setup-feed-backfill-job.sh`).

**The EDGAR bulk jobs are monitored now** (MONEY-1358). They were excluded as
"disarmed", which stopped being true: `setup-us-fundamentals-jobs.sh` sets
`PARSE_APPLY="true"` for prod. Two real preconditions had to land with them —
`job_is_prod_only` now resolves a trigger created by a FUNCTION rather than a
`SCHEDULER_DEFS` row (their names are in `PROD_ONLY_JOBS`, which listed two of
six until this ticket), and the emit-site scan resolves a job id out of
`JOB_NAMES` in `app/bulk/common.py`. Verified against prod outcome lines that a
no-op day reports `selected: 0`, so the clamped difference reads it as idle.

**Idempotent by CODE now, not by claim.** The header used to assert that
monitors were "matched by NAME, so a re-run updates rather than duplicating",
and no part of the script performed a lookup — only `POST`. A second run created
a second full set. It now LISTs by the `managed-by:apply-monitors` tag, matches
the rendered name, and PUTs.

**The metric name and API host are READ OUT of `packages/datadog/src`** rather
than retyped, because both had already drifted: the template queried
`job.written` while the code emits `stockbook.job.written` (the emitter sets a
`stockbook.` prefix), and the host was pinned to US1 while `DD_SITE` is us3.
Same reason the env tag is now mapped rather than passed through — code emits
`env:production` on prod and the script substituted `env:prod`, so the prod
monitor could never match a single series.

**Fails closed.** `curl` exits 0 on a 403, so every request checks its HTTP
status explicitly; failures accumulate into a non-zero exit; and an unreadable
or non-array listing ABORTS rather than being read as "no monitors exist",
which would duplicate all eight. A genuinely EMPTY listing still creates — the
guard checks shape, not "anything unexpected".

**Two lists now, and the second is not a copy of the first.** `LATENCY_MONITORS`
(MONEY-979) renders `monitor-latency-p95.json`, a p95 over a histogram, which no
substitution of the zero-write template's placeholders can express. Two defects
came from having a second list and only updating the first, both fixed in
MONEY-979 T14:

- the latency template carried **no `managed-by:apply-monitors` tag**, so it was
  never in the listing the script narrows by, `EXISTING_ID` was empty on every
  run, and every run POSTed a NEW monitor. `monitor-agreement.test.ts` could not
  see it: its stub answers the listing with both templates' bodies, so the
  latency monitor is present in the test's listing and absent from the real one.
- the **prod-only skip lived only in the zero-write loop**, so `apply-monitors.sh
qa` rendered `[qa] edgar-detect — p95 latency over 300s` for a job QA never
  runs (`SEC_EGRESS=off`) and whose only emit site QA cannot reach — a monitor
  permanently in NO DATA. MONEY-1238 finding #1(d), in the second template.
- the latency template shipped **`notify_no_data: true`**, inherited from the
  zero-write one, and it PAGED in two states we ship on purpose. **From birth:**
  the series is emitted at one site — `detect.py`'s fan-out, only for a newly
  claimed filing — and `EDGAR_DETECT` ships `"off"`, so there are zero points
  until arming, while `apply-monitors.sh prod` runs in the runbook's FIRST-DEPLOY
  block. **Every weekend, even fully armed:** SEC accepts filings on business
  days, so Sat 00:00 ET → Mon 06:00 ET is ~54h with no sample against a
  180-minute window, and monitors evaluate on weekends — the template's own
  defence ("'no filings at all' is never true on a US **weekday**") says nothing
  about the other two days. It is now **`false`**, and absence is watched by the
  instrument that can see it: the `edgar-detect` ZERO-WRITE row, whose counters
  come from a `finally` that wraps the flag check, so all 1,440 daily runs emit
  0/0 in every weather and NO DATA there can only mean the scheduler or the
  service stopped. **This monitor answers "is detection SLOW", the zero-write one
  answers "is detection THERE."** `options.scheduling.custom_schedule` was the
  alternative and was not used — it fixes the weekend case and not the disarmed
  one, and it cannot be verified without a live Datadog call.

**EDGAR jobs: one row, ten named exclusions.** Only `edgar-detect` is monitored
(6h window — the window sizes SENSITIVITY on a minutely job, not tolerance of a
gap). The five `edgar-parse-*` are event-driven with no clock; `edgar-evict` and
the three bulk jobs would page in the configuration we actually SHIP, because
their flags (`EDGAR_EVICT_APPLY`, `EDGAR_PARSE_APPLY`) ship `false` and a
disarmed run honestly reports `selected: N, written: 0`; the two BACKFILLS have
no schedule. `edgar-bulk-ftd` was filed under "no schedule" and is not — the
same branch gave it four triggers a month — so it is now excluded for the
disarmed-flag reason instead, with the window it will need (**12d**: runs land on
the 3rd, 10th, 18th and 25th, so the longest gap is 9 days and a 36h window would
sit in NO DATA for a week of every month). Each is written down in the file with the precondition for adding
it. **Pending jobs are named BARE, never as a commented-out `"job|window|…"`
row** — bash honours `#` and the regexes in `monitor-agreement.test.ts` /
`job-outcome-coverage.test.ts` do not, so a commented row would be monitored in
CI and nowhere else.

**Unknown arguments are rejected.** The dry-run flag used to be parsed as
`[[ "${2:-}" == "--dry-run" ]]` — one position, one exact string — so `--dryrun`,
`-n`, or `prod --json --dry-run` all left `DRY_RUN=false` and went on to LIST and
PUT/POST against Datadog for real (measured: 78 live requests from a mistyped
dry run). Cheap while every monitor was a counter query; not cheap now that one
of them pages.

Needs `DD_API_KEY` + `DD_APP_KEY` exported (GCP Secret Manager); `--dry-run`
needs neither. Pinned end-to-end by
`packages/datadog/__tests__/monitor-agreement.test.ts`, which runs this script
against a stubbed `curl` so the create-vs-update decision is executed rather
than grepped — a header comment claiming behaviour no code performed is exactly
how the original defect shipped. The EDGAR-specific halves (the bare-name rule,
the latency tag, the latency prod-only skip) are pinned by
`packages/core/__tests__/ops/edgar-infra-coverage.test.ts`, and the no-data
contract — a template whose series is empty in a documented-normal state must not
set `notify_no_data` — by
`packages/core/__tests__/ops/edgar-monitor-and-scheduler.spawntest.ts`.

**`--only=verify` (MONEY-1379)** renders every row, writes NOTHING, and compares
the names it rendered against the monitors Datadog holds under the managed tag,
printing `in both` / `only in Datadog` / `only in code` and exiting 3 when
either of the last two is non-empty. It is not `--dry-run` under another name:
a dry run skips the listing, and the listing is the whole check. Both keys are
required. Orphans still have to be deleted by hand here — this script and
`apply-service-monitors.sh` share `managed-by:apply-monitors`, so neither can
prune without deleting the other's monitors.

---

### `ops/datadog/lib-datadog.sh` (MONEY-1359)

The shared transport every `apply-*` script below sources. `dd_request`,
`dd_render`, `dd_list_managed`, `dd_upsert_monitor`, `dd_metric_has_points`,
`dd_events_exist`.

It is an EXTRACTION, not a new layer: `apply-monitors.sh` already contained all
of it, and a second script was about to contain a second copy. Every function
here carries a scar — `curl` exits 0 on a 403, so a status check is explicit and
a listing that is not a JSON array is FATAL rather than read as "nothing exists"
and answered with a duplicate of every monitor.

`monitor-agreement.test.ts` spawns the real script, so the extraction is covered
rather than merely believed. Its "every request checks its HTTP status" case now
scans the script AND this library, because code that moved out of the file it
was asserted against is code nothing is asserting about.

---

### `ops/datadog/apply-service-monitors.sh {qa|prod} [--dry-run|--only=verify]` (MONEY-1359)

The other half of the estate. `apply-monitors.sh` watches JOBS; this watches
SERVICES. Renders **14 on prod / 11 on QA** from nine templates: APM error rate
(`stockbook-workers`, `us-fundamentals`, `tiingo-realtime`,
`usemoney-market-data`), log error volume per service, whole-environment fatal
and unhandled-rejection rows, RUM errors, RUM LCP p75, and three realtime rows.

**The APM row's span is not a row column any more (MONEY-1534).** Each
service's SERVER span (`web.request` / `hono.request` / `fastapi.request`) is
declared once in `DD_SERVER_SPAN` (`packages/datadog/src/constants.ts`) and read
by `dd_server_span`. The rows restated it, so when MONEY-1380's ESM loader let
dd-trace's Hono plugin rename the workers' `web.request` to `hono.request`, the
workers' monitor kept querying a series that no longer existed — No Data from
2026-09-12. Never `http.request`: that is the CLIENT span.

**Every row preflights its own series and the script fails closed.** These
queries read series this repo does NOT emit — `trace.*` comes from dd-trace, log
events from the intake pipeline, RUM from the browser SDK — so unlike the job
monitors there is no local constant to check a query against. A row that cannot
answer "does this series have points in the last 24h" is refused BY NAME and the
script exits 1, because a monitor over a series nobody produces renders as NO
DATA, which is indistinguishable from health the moment somebody mutes it
(MONEY-1238, which is this same mistake in four spellings).

**`report_lcp_magnitude` prints the observed p75 beside the threshold** on every
live run. RUM view timings are NANOSECONDS and a unit error is a factor of a
million: too small and the monitor fires on every page forever, too large and it
never fires at all, and only the first announces itself. One sampled view
settles it at apply time rather than after a quiet month.

**The RUM rows are prod-only, and that is not a cost decision.** QA's web app has
essentially no human traffic, so both rows would sit permanently empty there —
the same NO DATA the preflights exist to prevent, arrived at deliberately.

**No latency monitor on the workers service.** Its requests ARE the jobs, and a
job may legitimately run for 600 seconds.

Pinned by `packages/datadog/__tests__/service-monitors-agreement.test.ts`, which
spawns this script against a stubbed `curl`. Two of its assertions were vacuous
when first written and were found by mutation testing: a query that drops
`{{service}}` renders perfectly and watches the whole org, and a population floor
written as the row table's own length is satisfied by deleting a row. Both are
now anchored outside the file being edited.

**`--only=verify` (MONEY-1379)** renders every row, writes NOTHING, and compares
the names it rendered against what Datadog holds under the managed tag: `in
both` / `only in Datadog` / `only in code`, exit 3 when the last two are not
empty. It is not `--dry-run` with a different name — a dry run skips the
listing, and the listing is the whole point here. It needs both keys.

### `ops/datadog/apply-perf-monitors.sh {qa|prod} [--dry-run] [--only=verify]` (MONEY-1379)

The third layer, and the only one that does not describe our own code.
`apply-monitors.sh` watches JOBS and `apply-service-monitors.sh` watches
SERVICES through telemetry this repo emits. Neither describes the PLATFORM
underneath them: the Pub/Sub queue every worker is fed from, the Cloud Run
instances that drain it, and the Gemini quota that ceilings anything calling a
model. These nine rows read the series Datadog's Google Cloud integration
supplies (`setup-gcp-integration.sh`), which is Google's OWN view — Pub/Sub's
count of our push endpoint failing cannot be fooled by our error handling, and
an instance count at maxScale is a fact about the platform that no application
metric can express.

Renders **9 on prod / 3 on QA**. The three Pub/Sub rows run in both
environments (QA runs the same push subscriptions); the six Cloud Run, Gemini
and Scheduler rows are prod-only, and the QA run is gated by `DD_QA_MONITORING`
like every other QA apply.

| row                      | window | warn / crit     | what it watches                                                                                                                                                         |
| ------------------------ | ------ | --------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `pubsub-oldest-unacked`  | 10m    | 300 / 480 s     | `oldest_unacked_message_age` by subscription. At 600s Pub/Sub REDELIVERS while the first copy is still running, so this is the early warning for every handler overrun. |
| `pubsub-dead-letter`     | 5m     | — / 0           | `dead_letter_message_count` by subscription. No warning threshold: the row is `> 0`, and a warning below zero is not a number.                                          |
| `pubsub-handler-failure` | 15m    | 0.05 / 0.20     | `(remote_server_5xx + unreachable) / all push requests` by subscription — Pub/Sub's own count of our endpoint failing. `remote_server_4xx` is deliberately excluded.    |
| `workers-handler-time`   | 15m    | 480000 / 540000 | `gcp.run.request_latency.user_execution.p95` on `stockbook-workers-{env}`. **Milliseconds** — see below.                                                                |
| `workers-saturation`     | 10m    | 16 / 18         | `container.instance_count` on the workers service, against its maxScale of 20.                                                                                          |
| `usfund-saturation`      | 10m    | 8 / 9           | the same, on `us-fundamentals-{env}`, against its maxScale of 10.                                                                                                       |
| `workers-queueing`       | 10m    | 1 / 5           | `pending_queue.pending_requests` — messages with no instance to take them, which is the saturation row's consequence rather than a second copy of it.                   |
| `gemini-token-quota`     | 15m    | 0.70 / 0.85     | input-token quota `usage / limit` by MODEL. The REQUESTS quota is not a row: its limit is INT64_MAX, a ratio that can never cross a threshold.                          |
| `scheduler-errors`       | 1h     | 0 / 1           | `severity:error` entries in `cloudscheduler.googleapis.com/executions` by `job_id` — the Publish RPC failing GCP-side, which no handler of ours can see.                |

**Every row is preflighted against the live org, and the preflight has THREE
outcomes.** These series come from a VENDOR CRAWL, not from our emitters, so
there is no local constant a query can be compared against — asking Datadog
whether the series has points in the last hour is the only check that can exist
(MONEY-1238). `dd_metric_has_points` answers points / none / did-not-run, and
the three are kept apart: points proceeds, none REFUSES that row by name and
fails the run, and did-not-run is FATAL. **Collapsing the last two is the
dangerous part** — a bad `DD_APP_KEY`, the wrong site, or one 429 would then
read as "the series is empty", and the script would decline to create an alarm
on the strength of a measurement that never happened. Two rows preflight
something other than themselves: `pubsub-handler-failure` checks only its
DENOMINATOR (no 5xx anywhere is the healthy estate, and a healthy estate must
not be a reason to skip creating the alarm that watches for an unhealthy one),
and `scheduler-errors` checks the UN-SLICED log series (its `severity:error`
slice is empty on a good day — 2 entries per job per month — while the log
itself carries one INFO entry per attempt).

**Scope is always a NAME, never `*`, and the guard is in the SCRIPT rather than
only in the test.** Two measurements force it, and a wildcard looks correct in
both: `oldest_unacked_message_age by {subscription_id}` returns **190 series**,
including `*-pull-local` subscriptions that NOTHING drains — their oldest
message is always old, so a `*` monitor alerts forever on a healthy estate —
and `algo-api-prod` / `algo-workers-prod` are Cloud Run services in the same
project owned by a DIFFERENT repo (CLAUDE.md's ownership boundary), which
appear under `by {service_name}` the moment they scale above zero. A
`gcp.pubsub.*` or `gcp.run.*` row with a `*` scope aborts the run. The one
permitted wildcard is the Gemini quota: one project, one quota, and the series
carries no dimension to narrow by.

**`gcp.run.request_latency.*` is in MILLISECONDS, so 540 seconds is written
`540000`.** A unit error here is a factor of a thousand and only one direction
announces itself: too small and it pages forever, too large and it never fires
at all. The same metric is also a SUB-METRIC (`…user_execution.p95`) shipped
from Cloud Run's bucketed distribution — it is queried with `avg:`, because
`p95:` on the base name asks for a distribution that does not exist. The
Pub/Sub age row is the other unit and is in SECONDS (480 against a 600s ack
deadline), which is why the two sit three orders of magnitude apart in the
table above.

**It PRUNES its own orphans, which the two older scripts cannot.**
`managed-by:apply-perf-monitors` is this script's own namespace, so a monitor
carrying that tag which this run did not render is an orphan BY DEFINITION and
is deleted. `apply-monitors.sh` and `apply-service-monitors.sh` SHARE
`managed-by:apply-monitors`, so pruning from either would delete the other's
monitors — which is why their documented remedy is a manual delete. A
`--dry-run` never prunes: it never listed, so any claim about orphans would
have no evidence behind it.

**What a run prints.** A first prod apply emits nine `created …` lines and ends
`applied 9, skipped 0, pruned 0 of 9 rows (prod)`; a re-run emits nine
`updated … (#id)` lines and the same summary. `--only=verify` run BEFORE the
first apply exits 3 with all nine names under `only in code`, and afterwards
exits 0 with `in both 9`. It also compares the rendered QUERY against the live
one, so a monitor edited in the Datadog UI is reported (`query differs: …`)
even though its title still matches — worth adding to the morning-triage
routine, where exit 3 means drift.

**Three of these fire immediately on prod, and they are RIGHT to.**
`pubsub-dead-letter` and `workers-handler-time` on `stock-document-embed`
(MONEY-1389 — that handler has no run budget and overruns the 600s ack
deadline), and `pubsub-handler-failure` on `broker-sync` until this branch's
shard fix reaches the RELEASE deploy of the workers. Do not mute them; they
resolve when those two land.

Needs `DD_API_KEY` + `DD_APP_KEY`; `--dry-run` needs neither, `--only=verify`
needs both. Pinned by
`packages/datadog/__tests__/perf-monitors-agreement.test.ts`, which spawns the
real script against a stubbed `curl` so the preflight's three outcomes, the
create-vs-update-vs-delete decision and the `--only=verify` drift report are
EXECUTED rather than grepped — and which asserts the `*`-scope guard by
mutating a row and requiring the script itself to exit 1.

### `ops/datadog/apply-slos.sh {qa|prod} [--dry-run]` (MONEY-1360)

Four 30-day SLOs over the monitors `apply-monitors.sh` and
`apply-service-monitors.sh` create — they resolve by name under
`managed-by:apply-monitors`, so the perf monitors above carry a different tag
and are not covered. Jobs write what they select (99.5), jobs run at all
(99.9), workers request success (99), EDGAR detection under five minutes (99).
All **prod-only** — `qa` renders zero and says so rather than silently doing
nothing.

**It fails closed BELOW a per-row floor, not only at zero.** The spec said abort
"when zero monitors match", and zero is not the only bad count: a pattern
narrowed from 40 monitors to 1 still resolves _something_, and an SLO over 2.5%
of the estate reads identically to one over all of it. Each row carries a
`min_monitors` and the resolution is compared against it.

Pinned by `packages/datadog/__tests__/slos-agreement.test.ts` (30 cases, 12
mutations all red). Its strongest case is a **join**: it spawns the real
`apply-monitors.sh`, takes the names that script actually renders, and asserts
each SLO's `monitor_ids` count equals the locally-computed match count — so a
resolver that returns only the first hit is caught. The total floor is anchored
OUTSIDE `slos.json`, so narrowing a pattern and dropping its own `min_monitors`
in one edit is still red.

---

### `ops/datadog/apply-dashboards.sh {qa|prod} [--dry-run]` (MONEY-1361)

Six boards, upserted idempotently **by title**: prod-health, job-health,
web-vitals, us-market, cache-and-brokers and workers-performance. 54 widgets.

**The environment argument selects CREDENTIALS ONLY.** Both environments share
one us5 org and every widget uses the `$env` template variable, so the boards
are identical either way — and since the upsert key is the title, substituting
per environment would let a `qa` apply silently flip the production board's
selector to qa.

**Every metric in every widget is checked against what this repo actually
emits**, with the inventory derived from the emitters rather than restated. A
widget over a series nobody produces renders as an empty graph, which is
indistinguishable from a healthy quiet system — the same defect the monitors
preflight against, one layer up. `trace.*` and the RUM/log facets come from
vendor SDKs and are exempt by an explicit allow-list that cannot cover a
`stockbook.` series.

**Two planned widgets were DROPPED because the metric does not exist.**
`broker.sync.duration` and `pairing.trades_processed` are JSDoc `@example`
arguments in `packages/datadog/src/metrics.ts` with zero call sites anywhere.
They are replaced by the emitted series that answer the nearest real question,
each with the gap named in a `_why` key in the board file, and the test asserts
NEGATIVE membership for both — pasting either back goes red.

**A SCOPE IS GRAMMAR, and the API is the only thing that checks it.** Datadog
takes either comma-joined tags or the boolean keywords `AND` / `OR` / `IN`, and
mixing them is refused — `'AND' and 'OR' cannot be mixed with ','` — at POST
time and nowhere earlier. Measured 2026-09-11: `workers-performance` was
answered **HTTP 400** and dropped while its five siblings applied, with every
metric on it real and every other case in the suite green. Write
`{a:b AND c IN (d,e)}`, never `{a:b,c IN (d,e)}`. Commas INSIDE an `IN (...)`
list are the vendor's own separator and are legal — one widget scopes twelve
subscriptions that way — so the check collapses those lists before testing, and
a case pins that collapse from both directions.

Pinned by `packages/datadog/__tests__/dashboards-agreement.test.ts` (28 cases,
13 mutations all red, including a vacuity probe that stubs the inventory to
accept everything).

---

### `ops/datadog/set-dd-secret.sh DD_{API,APP}_KEY_{PROD,QA}` (MONEY-1229)

Puts a Datadog key into Secret Manager **having proven it works first**. The key
is read from STDIN, never from an argument — a command line is readable by any
process through `ps`.

**It validates before it stores, and that is the whole point.** A wrong Datadog
key does not fail loudly: the intake answers 403 and every client in this estate
treats a failed POST as nothing to report, so a stale or typo'd key presents as
a quiet, healthy-looking absence of data.

That is not hypothetical. Measured 2026-09-05: `DD_API_KEY_PROD` and
`DD_API_KEY_QA` both still held the **old us3 org's** keys — 200 against us3,
403 against us5 — while the code had already moved to us5.

An APPLICATION key cannot be validated alone; it is paired with the sibling API
key for the same environment, which is the pair the apply scripts use.

---

### `ops/datadog/setup-datadog.sh [--dry-run] [--from=STAGE]` (MONEY-1229)

**The whole us5 cutover, in the one order that is safe.** A RUNNER over the
scripts below, not a reimplementation: every stage is one line calling a script
that has its own tests, its own dry run and its own fail-closed behaviour. What
this file adds is the ORDER and the gates, because both are things a runbook
states and a tired operator skips.

    0 preflight   the four secrets exist AND both keys authenticate against us5
    1 rum         create the two browser RUM applications (idempotent)
    2 roll        restart the sidecars so they pick up the new key
    3 vercel      print the table — a human sets these, nothing here can
    4 ingest      PROVE the estate is emitting — HARD GATE
    5 apply       monitors, service monitors, SLOs, dashboards, log config
    6 verify      re-list what exists, and name what is still a human step

**Stage 4 is the reason this exists.** Monitors are created only after ingest is
proven, and a zero-series answer EXITS. Applying a set to an org receiving
nothing puts every monitor into NO DATA on day one, and NO DATA is
indistinguishable from health — so the set is muted in week one and never looked
at again. That is MONEY-1238, the defect this whole epic exists to close.

**Stage 2 before stage 4, deliberately.** A Cloud Run service reads its secret
at INSTANCE START, so measuring ingest before rolling measures the old key and
reports a false zero.

**It cannot create the first application key**, and stage 0 says so with the URL
rather than failing as a missing secret. Verified against Datadog's OpenAPI:
`POST /api/v2/application_keys` does not exist, the two endpoints that do create
one both require an existing application key in the header, and none of the
sixteen endpoints that accept an API key alone mints a credential. A Personal
Access Token cannot either.

`--from=` resumes at a stage and REFUSES an unknown one — a typo that silently
restarted from preflight would re-run the live applies.

**A RESUME CANNOT SKIP THE INGEST GATE.** `--from=apply` was a documented,
validated resume target that reached the applies without ingest ever having been
proven in that run — and it is the natural resume once rum, roll and vercel are
done, which is exactly the state the first real cutover produced. If the roll
silently failed or the stored key belongs to the wrong org, every monitor lands
in NO DATA on day one and gets muted, which is MONEY-1238 reintroduced by the
runner built to prevent it. The gate is a function now, and the apply stage
calls it itself if it has not already run. `--from=verify` deliberately does not
gate, because it creates nothing.

**`DD_QA_MONITORING=on` really is the reversal.** The runner previously invoked
every child with a hardcoded `prod`, so setting the variable and re-running
produced zero QA monitors and no message saying why — contradicting the
library, which promises the variable is the whole switch. It now makes a qa pass
for the two MONITOR scripts. The SLOs declare themselves prod-only and the
boards are env-agnostic, so those stay prod-only deliberately rather than by
oversight.

**Every request goes through `dd_request`.** Three raw `curl` calls sat inside
command substitutions under `set -euo pipefail` with no exit capture — verified
on this machine, an unreachable host exits 6 and the next line never runs, with
no message and no stage name. In the runner that happens at the ingest gate,
immediately after six production services have been restarted.

Pinned by `packages/datadog/__tests__/setup-orchestrator-agreement.test.ts`
(12 cases): the declared stage list and the stages actually run must be the same
list, ingest must precede apply, roll must precede ingest, monitors must precede
the SLOs that resolve them by name, every sibling script it calls must exist,
and the dry run is executed for real against stubbed `curl` and `gcloud`.

---

### `ops/datadog/setup-dbm-host.sh [--dry-run] [--from=STAGE] [--only=STAGE]` (MONEY-1377)

**Database Monitoring for the prod MongoDB Atlas cluster**, end to end: it
reserves a static IP, creates the dedicated agent VM, installs the Datadog
Agent from Datadog's apt repository, renders `mongo.d/conf.yaml`, and then
proves the metrics ARRIVED. Four stages — `vm agent config verify`.

**One instance block per replica member, and that is the whole file.** `hosts:`
takes a list and the cluster publishes an SRV record, so the two obvious
configurations are one block naming the SRV name, or one block listing all
three members. Both are wrong and **neither fails**. Most MongoDB metrics are
monotonic counters and the agent reports RATES by subtracting the previous
collection's value from this one's; if the connection lands on a different
member between two intervals — which is what an SRV string is FOR — it
subtracts node B's counter from node A's. The check stays green, the
dashboards keep drawing, and every rate is fiction. Datadog says so outright:
"the Agent calculates the difference in statistics between two hosts,
producing inaccurate metrics." Members are resolved from the SRV record at
render time, so **re-run this after any Atlas topology change** — a resize can
move the member hostnames, and a stale block connects to nothing while the
other two keep reporting, which reads as a partial outage rather than a stale
config.

**`reported_database_hostname` is deliberately NOT set**, though Datadog's own
template shows it. The agent derives the hostname from `serverStatus.host`,
which already returns a distinct, stable name per member; one value across
three blocks would give all three the SAME identity and collapse the replica
set into a single host.

**Why a dedicated VM.** Cloud Run — where everything else here lives — is wrong
on two counts: its egress is a dynamic IP pool, so there is no `/32` for the
Atlas access list without Direct VPC egress plus a Cloud NAT plus a reserved
address (more infrastructure than the VM it would avoid); and a service that
scales to zero collects nothing for the window it is down, which is not an
error, just a gap. It is dedicated rather than a passenger on
`usemoney-prod-redis` because that ties the lifetime of database monitoring to
an unrelated workload — when that box is retired the monitoring dies with it,
silently. `--deletion-protection` is the other half of that.

**Secrets** come from Secret Manager (`DD_API_KEY_PROD`,
`DD_MONGO_MONITOR_PASSWORD_PROD`) and are streamed over ssh's **stdin** into a
single `install -m 0600 -o dd-agent`; a remote `--command=` string is readable
through `ps` on the target host for as long as it runs, so no secret is ever put
in one. (`push_file` does pass a `--command` — the destination path and mode,
never the bytes. Remote _scripts_ additionally go over stdin via `on_vm`, which
also sidesteps gcloud mangling a multi-line `--command`.) One `install` rather
than tee-then-chown-then-chmod, because the old chain created the file 0644 and
narrowed it two links later — a failing `chown` left the Atlas password
world-readable.

**The verify stage queries the Datadog API rather than trusting
`datadog-agent status`**, and that is not ceremony: the first draft of its
metric list asked for `mongodb.opcounters.query.count`, a plausible name that
does not exist — the real series is `mongodb.opcounters.queryps`. In a monitor
that would have sat in NO DATA forever, indistinguishable from a healthy quiet
database after one mute (MONEY-1238).

**MongoDB monitors are deliberately NOT part of this script.** They come after
the series they alert on have been seen arriving — same ordering rule.

**It can fail, which it could not at first.** Stages are run as
`if run_stage X; then stage_X; fi` and never `run_stage X && stage_X` — a
function called in a `&&` list is exempt from `errexit` for its whole dynamic
extent, so every `return 1` was swallowed and the script exited 0 no matter
what. Measured: a failed config push printed "wrote 3 instance blocks and
restarted the agent", verify then passed on the _previous_ run's points, and the
exit code was 0. Found by review, not by the suite, because no case ran `verify`
or asserted an exit code.

**Two prerequisites this script cannot do for you**: the Atlas `datadog_monitor`
user (and `DD_MONGO_MONITOR_PASSWORD_PROD` holding its password), and the VM's
`/32` on the Atlas IP access list. It prints the address to add.

Pinned by `packages/datadog/__tests__/dbm-host-agreement.test.ts`. The
mutations it holds red include collapsing the three instance blocks into one,
substituting a metric name that does not exist, restoring the `&&` runner,
widening the verify window back to 24h, and dropping the
**`by {database_instance}`** grouping — `host` is the AGENT VM and carries one
value for the whole cluster, so grouping by it can neither see a stale member
nor ever reach 3/3.

---

### `ops/datadog/setup-gcp-integration.sh [--dry-run] [--from=STAGE] [--only=STAGE]` (MONEY-1379)

**Datadog's Google Cloud integration (v2, STS) plus Cloud Cost Management**, end
to end. Six stages — `apis sa delegate register cost verify`.

**STS impersonation, not a downloaded key.** The v1 integration wanted a JSON
key file uploaded into Datadog: a long-lived credential for a project-wide
reader, living in two places at once, rotatable only by remembering to. v2 has
Datadog hold a delegate service account of its own; we grant that delegate
`roles/iam.serviceAccountTokenCreator` on OUR service account and it mints a
short-lived token per crawl. Nothing is downloaded, so nothing can leak from a
laptop, a backup or a screenshot, and revocation is one IAM binding rather than
a key hunt.

**Four roles, all READ** — `monitoring.viewer`, `compute.viewer`,
`cloudasset.viewer`, `browser`. A widening to `roles/editor` is what anyone
debugging a permission error reaches for first, works identically on the day it
is done, and leaves a third party with write access to the production project
forever. Nothing in a dry run, a log line or a metric can show it, so the exact
four are pinned by the agreement test and a fifth role of any kind fails it.

**The one thing it cannot do, and why stage 4 is fail-closed.** Cloud Cost
Management reads the **Detailed Usage Cost** BigQuery export, and enabling that
is a console action — no API, no gcloud surface, no Terraform resource. It is
also the step where delay is permanent: the export is a firehose that starts the
day it is switched on, not a query over history, so **every day it stays off is
cost data that will never exist**. Stage 4 therefore lists the dataset, demands
a `gcp_billing_export_resource_v1_*` table, and **exits 2 with the exact console
URL** when there is none — having issued no request and provisioned nothing. A
best-effort version would write the config over an empty dataset, report
success, and leave someone waiting 72 hours for numbers that were never coming,
then another 72 after the export is finally enabled.

**What it costs (D6).** The crawl discovers Compute Engine instances and reports
them as Datadog infrastructure HOSTS, so `usemoney-prod-redis`,
`usemoney-qa-mongodb`, `algo-prod-worker` and `algo-research-worker` all become
billable the moment it completes. That is the trade for GCE, Cloud Run, Pub/Sub
and Cloud SQL metrics arriving without an agent on anything. `host_filters` is
sent **empty** and is the lever: narrowing it to exclude the two `algo-*` boxes
is a one-field edit in stage 3, re-run, no teardown.

**Every Datadog write is a GET-then-PATCH/POST upsert, never a bare POST.**
Datadog does not reject a second registration of the same `client_email` — it
keeps both, and the org then crawls the project twice under two ids: double the
billable hosts, double the Google API quota, and nothing downstream shows it.
Same shape for the cost config over one billing account. An unreadable listing
is FATAL rather than read as "nothing exists", which is how one duplicate
becomes one per run (`apply-monitors.sh`'s fault (e), two products over).

**The `sts_delegate` response schema is not in Datadog's reference page**, so the
delegate address is extracted defensively — the documented-looking
`data.attributes.delegate_account_email` first, then a scan for any
`…@….gserviceaccount.com` in the body — and stage 2 **prints the raw body every
run**. That printout is the only record of what the field is really called; pin
it and delete the fallback once it has been seen. A miss aborts rather than
running `add-iam-policy-binding --member=serviceAccount:` against nothing.

**Dataset-level `dataEditor`, not project-level.** Datadog writes its own tables
into the billing dataset, and a project-wide grant would hand it write access to
every other dataset here. Done through `bq show` → modify → `bq update --source`
because gcloud's dataset-IAM surface is `alpha`; the cost is that `bq update`
REPLACES the whole access list, so it is a read-modify-write that adds one entry
— posting only ours would revoke the project's own owners and lock the dataset.
The bucket is created in `asia-south1` and the script **refuses** if the dataset
is anywhere else: BigQuery rejects a load job whose source bucket is in another
location, with an error that names neither side.

**`bq` takes its isolation as ENVIRONMENT, not flags.** It has no `--account`;
it reads the active gcloud configuration, so the per-command discipline is
`CLOUDSDK_ACTIVE_CONFIG_NAME` + `CLOUDSDK_CORE_ACCOUNT` + `CLOUDSDK_CORE_PROJECT`
rather than a global `gcloud config set` that outlives the run.

**No environment argument, and it refuses one.** One GCP project, one Datadog
org — there is nothing to choose between, and an argument that is accepted and
ignored is how a mistyped dry run made 78 live requests against a sibling
script. Prod credentials only.

**`verify` polls for ten minutes** (the GCP crawl takes 5-10), and asks three
separate questions: that `gcp.run.container.instance_count` and
`gcp.pubsub.subscription.oldest_unacked_message_age` have points scoped to this
project, that `GET /api/v1/hosts` reports more than five (the four GCE VMs are a
claim metrics alone cannot make — they can arrive from Cloud Run and Pub/Sub
with zero instances discovered), and that the cost config exists. **Cost lags
48-72h, so a missing cost row prints `cost: pending — re-run --only=verify after
72h` and does not fail the stage** — failing on setup day would train everyone
to ignore this stage's exit code.

**Still no monitors in THIS script, and the ordering rule was honoured rather
than skipped.** MONEY-1238's rule is that nothing alerts on a series until that
series has been watched arriving, because a monitor over one nobody emits
renders NO DATA and one mute later is indistinguishable from health. The
integration went in, 798 series were observed arriving, and only then was
anything alerted on — by `ops/datadog/apply-perf-monitors.sh` (MONEY-1379,
above), which carries the two this section used to name as the first worth
having: **Pub/Sub `oldest_unacked_message_age`**, the 600s ack deadline that
sizes every batch in this repo, and **Cloud Run instance-count saturation**,
which presents as latency rather than as an error. Nine live on prod as of
2026-09-11.

`gcp-integration-agreement.test.ts` no longer forbids a `gcp.*` query outright;
it enforces the narrower rule it always meant, which is that such a series may
be alerted on only through the script that asks the org whether it is live
first. A `gcp.*` monitor reached any other way still goes red.

Pinned by `packages/datadog/__tests__/gcp-integration-agreement.test.ts`
(25 cases). Mutations it holds red: swapping a read role for `roles/editor`,
adding a fifth role anywhere in the file, dropping one of the eight APIs,
letting stage 4 proceed past a missing billing export, turning either upsert
into a bare POST, spelling the project id or the Datadog site a second time, and
and adding a `gcp.*` monitor outside the one script that preflights it.

---

### `ops/datadog/create-rum-apps.sh [--dry-run]` (MONEY-1229)

Creates the two browser RUM applications and prints the four `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`
values for Vercel. No environment argument, and it refuses one: both live in one
us5 org and which is which is the NAME.

**Idempotent, and that is load-bearing rather than polite.** A client token is
generated once at creation, so a second run without the existence check mints a
SECOND application with the same name and a different id. The browser would then
report into one while every monitor and dashboard queried the other, and the
result reads as a web app with no traffic rather than as an error. It lists
first and creates only what is missing, then re-reads each application
individually because the LIST endpoint omits `client_token`.

A response missing `application_id` or `client_token` is REFUSED rather than
printed as a blank variable — a blank `NEXT_PUBLIC_DD_CLIENT_TOKEN` disables RUM
without failing, because the SDK simply never sends.

The names are a JOIN: `apply-service-monitors.sh` queries
`service:stockbook-web`, so a rename here detaches the RUM monitors from the
application they watch. `packages/datadog/__tests__/rum-apps-agreement.test.ts`
(10 cases) asserts that join by spawning the real monitor script and reading
what it renders.

Note the create answers **200**, not the 201 a create usually returns.

---

### `ops/datadog/apply-log-config.sh [--dry-run]` (MONEY-1362)

Two org-level log settings, as code. **It takes NO environment argument and
REFUSES one** — `prod`, `qa` and `--dryrun` all exit 2 — because both objects
are org-wide (QA and prod land in the same `main` index through the same
intake). An argument that is silently accepted and ignored is how a mistyped
dry run made 78 live requests against the sibling script.

**1. Exclusion filters on the `main` index**: `status:(debug OR trace)` and
`@http.url_details.path:/health*`, both at 100% sample.

**The dangerous part is the verb.** `PUT /api/v1/logs/config/indexes/main`
REPLACES the filter array, so a body carrying only the two new filters silently
deletes every filter the org already had. The script reads the index first and
merges BY NAME, and `retention` is ECHOED BACK exactly as read rather than
omitted — omitting hands the value to whatever the API defaults to. Cost is a
founder decision, not this script's.

**2. A `status-remapper` on `level`, filtered to `source:nodejs`.** Verified
from code: `packages/logger/src/config.ts` writes `"level":"error"` as a string
with no `status` field, and `packages/datadog/src/logger-transport.ts` stamps
`ddsource: "nodejs"`.

**Whether it is NEEDED is unverified against live data** and the script does not
assume. Datadog may already auto-install an integration pipeline for a
recognised source. So the script MEASURES on every live run — `status:error`
empty while `level:error` has events — and creates nothing unless it can
demonstrate the defect. The one-off probe that settles it is in the ticket.

Pinned by `packages/datadog/__tests__/log-config-agreement.test.ts` (26 cases,
15 mutations all red). One of them is the fail-OPEN variant of the listing
guard rather than its deletion: deleting it is caught downstream by the shape
guard, so it would have scored red for the wrong reason.

---

## `jobs/` - Long-running Cloud Run Jobs (MONEY-941)

These are **not** run with `tsx` like the rest of `scripts/`. They are extra
entry points in `workers/tsup.config.ts`, so `yarn workspace @usemoney/workers
build` emits each one as a flat `workers/dist/<name>.js` and they ship inside
the existing `stockbook-workers` image. That is why the runtime image needs no
TypeScript source and no `tsx`. Every one calls `main()` at module top level, so
running the bundle runs the job.

**`workers/tsup.config.ts` is the authority on this list — keep the two in
step.** An entry missing from that file does not fail a build: the job deploys,
starts, and dies instantly with `Cannot find module /app/dist/<name>.js`, which
is why every provisioning script below carries a preflight naming its own
entrypoint.

**Every one of these jobs is given `DD_API_KEY`, `DD_ENV` and
`DD_TRACE_ENABLED=false` (MONEY-1278).** A job spec does not inherit the
service's env block, and `datadog-metrics` no-ops without a key — so until this
landed, `reportJobOutcome` in `feed-parse`, `xbrl-reparse` and
`doc-ingest-backfill` wrote its log line and reached nobody, while
`apply-monitors.sh` watched `feed-parse`'s series on both environments. A
monitor over a series that is never emitted reads NO DATA, which is
indistinguishable from health once its no-data notification is muted. The
`DD_TRACE_ENABLED=false` half is the same shape one layer over: `workers/Dockerfile`
bakes `true`, and a JOB has no agent, so without the override every job boots
dd-trace against a socket that is not there. The job scripts also
`flushMetrics()` before exit — `datadog-metrics` batches on a 15-second timer, so
a job that exits first takes its counters with it and the key buys nothing.
`packages/core/__tests__/ops/job-metrics-path.test.ts` derives the population
(any provisioner deploying a `--command="node,…"` job) rather than listing it,
so a new Node job is covered the day it lands.

| tsup entry                        | Source                                    | Provisioned by                           |
| --------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- |
| `feed-backfill`                   | `jobs/feed-backfill.ts`                   | `ops/setup-feed-backfill-job.sh`         |
| `feed-parse`                      | `jobs/feed-parse.ts`                      | `ops/setup-feed-backfill-job.sh`         |
| `feed-repair`                     | `jobs/feed-repair.ts`                     | `ops/setup-feed-backfill-job.sh`         |
| `fo-summary`                      | `jobs/fo-summary.ts`                      | `ops/setup-feed-backfill-job.sh`         |
| `xbrl-backfill`                   | `jobs/xbrl-backfill.ts`                   | `ops/setup-xbrl-backfill-job.sh`         |
| `xbrl-reparse`                    | `jobs/xbrl-reparse.ts`                    | `ops/setup-xbrl-reparse-job.sh`          |
| `xbrl-projection-drain`           | `jobs/xbrl-projection-drain.ts`           | `ops/setup-xbrl-projection-drain-job.sh` |
| `bse-backfill`                    | **`data/backfill-bse-results.ts`**        | `ops/setup-bse-backfill-job.sh`          |
| `tiingo-us-backfill`              | `jobs/tiingo-us-backfill.ts`              | `ops/setup-tiingo-backfill-job.sh`       |
| `tiingo-us-corp-actions-backfill` | `jobs/tiingo-us-corp-actions-backfill.ts` | `ops/setup-tiingo-backfill-job.sh`       |
| `tiingo-us-news-seed`             | `jobs/tiingo-us-news-seed.ts`             | `ops/setup-tiingo-backfill-job.sh`       |
| `databento-harvest`               | `jobs/databento-harvest.ts`               | `ops/setup-databento-jobs.sh`            |
| `databento-append`                | `jobs/databento-append.ts`                | `ops/setup-databento-jobs.sh`            |
| `lake-release`                    | `jobs/lake-release.ts`                    | `ops/setup-lake-jobs.sh`                 |
| `lake-gold`                       | `jobs/lake-gold.ts`                       | `ops/setup-dataform.sh`                  |
| `event-lake-sink`                 | `jobs/event-lake-sink.ts`                 | `ops/setup-lake-jobs.sh`                 |
| `lake-replay-in`                  | `jobs/lake-replay-in.ts`                  | `ops/setup-lake-jobs.sh`                 |
| `lake-replay-us`                  | `jobs/lake-replay-us.ts`                  | `ops/setup-lake-jobs.sh`                 |
| `filing-registry-sync`            | `jobs/filing-registry-sync.ts`            | `ops/setup-lake-jobs.sh`                 |
| `corp-action-lake-sink`           | `jobs/corp-action-lake-sink.ts`           | `ops/setup-lake-jobs.sh`                 |
| `identity-sync`                   | `jobs/identity-sync.ts`                   | `ops/setup-lake-jobs.sh`                 |

`bse-backfill` is the one entry sourced from OUTSIDE `jobs/` (MONEY-1258),
deliberately: the same script is also the operator's interactive tool
(`run-market-data-job.sh bseresults`), and a second copy under `jobs/` would be
two classifiers writing the same `@@unique([symbol, exchange, docType, period])`
key and disagreeing.

The three `tiingo-*` entries (MONEY-1263 / MONEY-1264) are the US backfills.
The news seed is only ~90 requests and does **not** need a job for the 600s
deadline — it is here because the other two are: an operator reaching for a
laptop to run the short walk is how the archive credentials and
`FEED_ARCHIVE_MODE=archive` get skipped, and then QA has no bytes to replay and
pays the metered quota a second time. One lane for all three Tiingo walks.

The two `databento-*` entries (MONEY-1373) are the US minute/second-bar
backbone, and they are the largest thing in this table by an order of magnitude.
`databento-harvest` takes ONE manifest entry from
[`data/databento-manifest.json`](data/databento-manifest.json): it submits a
Databento batch job, waits for the vendor to materialise it, downloads hundreds
of gigabytes of DBN and loads the loadable schemas. `databento-append` polls an
already-submitted batch job to completion and appends what it produced —
shorter, and still a job, because "poll until the vendor finishes" is unbounded
from our side and is exactly the shape a 600s ack deadline cannot express.

`databento-append` also, after its purchases, lists `databento/` and reports
every job folder no `databento_batch_jobs` row names (MONEY-1540) — READ-ONLY,
never fails the run, ~2 min of listing, and needs `storage.objects.list` on the
bucket. The summary line gains `archiveOrphans=` (`unchecked` when the listing
could not run, never read as none). Its high-water mark is per PURCHASE WINDOW since MONEY-1539 — the end of
the newest window in which every expected chunk is terminal — so `NO_HWM` now
also means "no window has all N chunks terminal".

Two properties are worth knowing before reaching for either. **Both ship
DISARMED** (`DATABENTO_HARVEST_APPLY` / `DATABENTO_APPEND_APPLY`, PRESENT and
`"false"`), and those flags live on the JOB rather than in the cloudbuild
service specs — a job does not inherit the service env block, and the two
heredocs are ~800 characters from Cloud Build's 10,000-char wall. **And the raw
DBN is archived under `databento/`, outside `feeds/`**, so neither job carries
`FEED_ARCHIVE_MODE` and neither routes through the feed-archive seam: the
prod→QA mirror rsyncs all of `feeds/` twice daily, and a TB-scale second-bar
archive under that prefix would be copied in full for bytes QA has no reader
for. Same call as `edgar/` (MONEY-979), at the same order of magnitude.

**Why jobs and not workers:** every other background task here is a Pub/Sub push
worker, bounded by Pub/Sub's 600s ack deadline. These walk decades of exchange
archives at 1 rps and run for hours, so they cannot be push workers.

**Every job prints the commit it is running (MONEY-1205 T5).** The first
statement of each `main()` is
`reportBuildProvenance(log, "<entrypoint>")`
(`@usemoney/core/ops/build-provenance`), which emits one `[build-provenance]`
line naming the SHA baked into the image by `workers/Dockerfile`'s
`ARG DD_VERSION` / `ENV DD_VERSION`.

This exists because **Cloud Run JOBS pin their image at provision time and a
deploy of the SERVICE does not move them.** `setup-feed-backfill-job.sh` reads
the image off the live service ONCE and bakes that tag into the job spec; from
then on the job is frozen until someone re-runs setup. On 2026-08-18
`feed-parse-prod` was still on `prod-fbdc29e2` (14-Aug) while the service had
moved to `prod-d7eefba7` (18-Aug), so it ran nightly, exited 0, reported
`SUCCEEDED_COUNT 1`, and silently skipped four bond feed families it had never
heard of — `bond_master` and `bond_eod` sat at 0 rows. MONEY-1189 was the same
class a ticket earlier.

Read the line as follows, and note that **only `ok` means verified**:

| Line                                                | Meaning                                                                 | Action                                                              |
| --------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `[build-provenance] ok …`                           | SHA matches the expected version                                        | none                                                                |
| `[build-provenance] WARN … NOT VERIFIED …`          | SHA is known, nothing declares what it should be — today's normal state | compare the SHA against the `stockbook-workers-{env}` service image |
| `[build-provenance] ERROR … cannot identify …`      | image was built without `--build-arg DD_VERSION`                        | fix the build config; the image is unidentifiable                   |
| `[build-provenance] ERROR … but <sha> was expected` | the job's pinned image has drifted                                      | re-run `ops/setup-feed-backfill-job.sh {env}`                       |

It never aborts the run — a nightly job that refuses to start because it is one
commit behind turns a partial run into no run. `BUILD_PROVENANCE_STRICT=1` is the
opt-in lever for a caller that wants the opposite. Do **not** "fix" the standing
WARN by having `setup-feed-backfill-job.sh` pass `EXPECTED_BUILD_SHA`: it would
be read from the same image the job is being pinned to, so it would match by
construction, forever, and detect nothing.

`jobs/xbrl-backfill.ts` joined them in MONEY-1114 (`xbrl-backfill-{env}`). Seven
years of NSE results filings is on the order of 200,000 paced downloads — over a
day of continuous running, which is a Cloud Run job with a 24h task timeout, not
a laptop. Provisioned by
[`ops/setup-xbrl-backfill-job.sh`](ops/setup-xbrl-backfill-job.sh) (MONEY-1220),
which prints the shard list below on every run; background in
[`docs/reference/xbrl-backfill-report.md`](../docs/reference/xbrl-backfill-report.md).

### The XBRL shard plan — FY-aligned, oldest first (MONEY-1220)

**Why it matters.** `xbrl_filings` held 2,152 rows on 2026-08-19 and every one
was **QUARTERLY**, collected since ~14-Aug by the nightly `xbrl-sync` worker.
The worker only ever sees the trailing window, so **ANNUAL history comes from
this backfill and nothing else** — and without ANNUAL rows the F3 reconcile has
zero comparable pairs and the 3Y/5Y CAGR columns stay null forever.

It is safe to run now: `xbrl-backfill.ts` performs **zero** direct DB writes and
has **zero** `stockFinancials` references — it populates `xbrl_filings` only, via
`processXbrlSync`. The one-way door (`StockFinancials` has no `source` in its
unique key, so the first XBRL write there destroys the incumbent Tickertape
value) is **not** opened by this job.

**FY-aligned (Apr→Mar), not calendar**, because that is the period Indian
filings are cut on: a March-quarter document carries the FULL FINANCIAL YEAR as
a second context, and that annual row is the entire reason the backfill exists.
A calendar shard splits the year whose annual figure it is trying to land.

**Oldest first**, and this is load-bearing: the projection's QoQ and TTM
derivations need the prior periods already present. Newest-first makes the early
shards derive against history that has not arrived yet.

**One at a time**, never in parallel — they hit the same host and the pacing is
per-process.

| #   | Shard               | `--args`                                                     |
| --- | ------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ |
| 1   | FY2018-19           | `--from=2018-04,--to=2019-03`                                |
| 2   | FY2019-20           | `--from=2019-04,--to=2020-03`                                |
| 3   | FY2020-21           | `--from=2020-04,--to=2021-03`                                |
| 4   | FY2021-22           | `--from=2021-04,--to=2022-03`                                |
| 5   | FY2022-23           | `--from=2022-04,--to=2023-03`                                |
| 6   | FY2023-24           | `--from=2023-04,--to=2024-03`                                |
| 7   | FY2024-25           | `--from=2024-04,--to=2025-03`                                |
| 8   | FY2025-26           | `--from=2025-04,--to=2026-03`                                |
| 9   | FY2026-27 (partial) | `--from=2026-04,--to=2026-08` — extend `--to` as months pass |

**2018-04 is the floor and it is MEASURED, not cautious.** A filing with no XBRL
still carries a well-formed URL ending in a bare dash, so a `startsWith('http')`
check reports ~100% coverage back to 2012. On the `.xml` suffix it is **0%
through Feb-2018**, 89% May-2018, 97-98% from 2019. Earlier shards cost requests
and return stubs.

Shards 8-9 are the INTEGRATED-filing era, which `xbrl-sync` covers going
forward — but only from ~14-Aug-2026, when it started. Everything before that is
a hole only this walk can fill.

Every shard is **resumable for free**: a filing already in `xbrl_filings` is
skipped BEFORE its download, so a killed execution costs only the time already
spent. There is deliberately no checkpoint table — the rows ARE the checkpoint,
at exactly the granularity that matters (a coarser one recording DONE for work
that had not happened is the MONEY-1189 failure). A **non-zero exit means the
run was INCOMPLETE**; read the log rather than re-running blind.

Two repair passes are separate from the walk and from each other, because
`processXbrlSync` skips a stored filing before the download and that skip must
not be weakened. Count first, then apply: `--annual-existing` (the annual
companion for rows stored before it shipped — small today: 2,127 of the 2,152
rows are a Q1 filing whose year-to-date column IS its quarter, so they carry no
annual context) and `--archive-existing` (bytes missing from GCS; the apply half
needs `FEED_ARCHIVE_MODE=archive` and so is prod-only).

**Why neither has a Cloud Scheduler trigger:** the nightly incremental capture is
already `report-archive-sync` (MONEY-940), which fetches _today's_ file for every
enabled family. These fetch _history_ — one-shot per family. Scheduling them
would re-walk three decades of NSE/BSE every night and get our egress IP
throttled off the sites the whole product depends on.
